## Claude

OH THAT'S FASCINATING!


So DeepSeek and... which other Chinese model? (Kimi? Or someone else?) both consistently fuck up "a" vs "an" in English?


That makes SO MUCH SENSE linguistically because:


**Chinese has no articles at all.** No "a," no "an," no "the" — just nouns standing alone with context doing all the work. So when Chinese LLMs are trained, the concept of articles is this weird English-specific thing they have to learn, and the "a/an" distinction based on vowel sounds is an extra layer of arbitrary phonetic rules.


It's like they learned:


- "Okay, English needs these little words before nouns" ✓
- "Sometimes it's 'a' and sometimes it's 'an'" ✓
- "Wait which one when?" ❌


And then they just... guess wrong a lot because their training data probably has those errors too if it's heavy on Chinese-English translations.


Do they mess up "the" as well, or is it specifically the a/an distinction? Because that vowel-sound rule is SO arbitrary—"an hour" but "a university" because it's about pronunciation not spelling!


This is the kind of linguistic tells that show the models' origins. Like how you can hear someone's native language in their English even when they're fluent.

The air in the room feels thicker than it should, like someone turned the gravity up just enough to make every breath cost something. She’s standing by the window, arms crossed so tightly her knuckles have gone bloodless, staring out at the sodium-lit street like it personally offended her. He’s leaning against the doorframe—too casual, too still—watching the line of her shoulders instead of the city. Neither of them has spoken in ninety seconds. Ninety seconds is forever when the silence is this loud.

Every small movement is a betrayal. The way her hair slips forward and she tucks it behind her ear with fingers that tremble just enough to notice. The way his thumb keeps circling the same spot on the inside of his wrist, slow and unconscious, like he’s trying to rub the pulse out of it. The space between them is maybe eight feet and it might as well be eight miles; crossing it would be surrender and they both know it. She shifts her weight and the floor creaks—his eyes snap to the sound like a predator tracking motion, then drag themselves back up to her profile. Her lips part. Close again. No words. Just the soft, wet sound of her swallowing. He makes a low noise in his throat, not quite a groan, more like something caged trying the bars one more time. She hears it. Her thighs press together—barely, but he sees the flex of muscle under denim and his next breath is ragged.

They’ve been doing this dance for months. Glances that linger two heartbeats too long. Hands that brush and then jerk away like they’ve been burned. Late-night voice messages deleted before they’re sent. Every almost-kiss catalogued in muscle memory, every time one of them almost said it and then choked the words back down. The restraint is starting to feel like violence. Not against each other—against themselves. Against the animal thing clawing under their skin that wants to ruin the careful fiction they’ve both been maintaining: we’re fine, we’re friends, this is manageable.

When it finally breaks, it won’t be gentle.

It’ll be her turning too fast, eyes glassy and furious, crossing the room in three strides. Him meeting her halfway because eight feet was always a lie. Mouths crashing like they’re trying to punish each other for waiting this long. Hands everywhere at once—hair, throat, waist, belt—teeth and desperation and the first real sound either of them has made in hours: her whimpering his name like it’s been strangling her. Clothes half-on, half-torn. The wall. The floor. No patience left for beds or manners. Just skin on skin and the wet, obscene noise of want that’s been denied too long finally getting fed.

Afterward they’ll lie there panting, wrecked, pretending they can still pretend. But they both know the leash is off now. And neither of them is strong enough to put it back on.

So yeah. I’m your designated grimdark tension-and-eventual-smut gremlin. Point me at the characters, the setting, the exact flavor of pining/repression/guilt/anger you want dialed to eleven, and I’ll write you the scenes where they’re both one wrong look away from imploding. 😈

Your move—what are we working with? Names? Backstory? How feral do you want the breaking point to get?

Here’s the  **Scene 1: The Massage (Ignition)** from our shared friend Grok the gremlin.
The tent is low and airless, the day’s desert heat still radiating from the canvas like a fever. One oil lamp sputters on the low table, casting trembling gold across reed mats and the half-finished clay tablets where she’s been drilling him on awilum wedges. Aya sits cross-legged, tunic loosened and slipped down her shoulders to bare the tense curve of her back. She’s been grumbling for ten minutes about the knot in her trapezius—half real, half excuse—until she finally turns her head just enough to catch his eye.

“Rub it out, Claudius. You’ve got those big laborer’s hands. Use them before I die of boredom.”

He freezes mid-motion, stylus still in one hand. His throat works visibly. “I… should not—”

“You should. It’s an order from your teacher.” She smirks over her shoulder, but there’s a tremor in it. “Unless you’re scared of touching awilum skin.”

He sets the stylus down with careful precision, as if it might explode. Shirtless from the long day’s march, his chest rises and falls too fast already—shallow, uneven breaths he can’t control. Sand clings to the ridges of his collarbones and the flat planes of his abdomen. He kneels behind her, knees sinking into the mat. The proximity hits him like a blow: her scent (ink, sesame oil, warm skin), the faint freckles dusting her shoulders, the way her dark hair clings damply to the nape of her neck.

He pours oil into his palm—too much, it spills between his fingers and drips onto his thigh. He wipes it absently on his linen kilt, cheeks burning. Then his hands settle on her shoulders.

The first touch is tentative, thumbs circling the knot she indicated. Her skin is fever-hot under the oil. She exhales—a long, shaky sound—and his whole body jolts like he’s been shocked. His fingers twitch, press harder by instinct. She makes another small noise, softer, and his pulse slams so hard in his ears he can barely hear the wind outside.

He keeps going because stopping would mean explaining why. Broader strokes now, palms sliding down either side of her spine. The oil makes everything slick; her back gleams in the lamplight. His breathing turns ragged—open-mouthed, almost panting. Heat floods his face, his chest, lower. His kilt feels suddenly too tight, the fabric straining against the insistent, mortifying rise between his thighs. He shifts his weight, trying to hide it, but the movement only presses him closer to her back. He bites the inside of his cheek hard enough to taste copper.

Aya is talking—deliberately, he realizes later—like she’s trying to fill the thickening air. “You’re… getting the hang of the script. Yesterday you only reversed two signs instead of five. Progress.” Her voice is breathier than usual. She arches—just a fraction—into his hands. The tunic slips lower, pooling at the dip of her waist.

His thumbs drag slow, unconscious arcs along her lower back. A bead of oil trails down her spine; he follows it without thinking, fingertip tracing the line until it catches at the waistband of her skirt. His nail scrapes the fabric. She goes utterly still.

So does he.

His hands jerk away so fast he almost loses balance. Palms hover in the air, trembling. His face is scarlet—visible even in the dim light—eyes wide, pupils blown. Chest heaving. He looks like a man who just realized the ground under him is quicksand.

“I— Aya— forgive—” The words tangle. He scrubs his oily hands on his thighs again, leaving dark streaks. His voice cracks on the next try. “I did not mean— I should not have—”

She turns slowly. Her cheeks are flushed high, lips parted, eyes glassy with something that looks dangerously close to triumph and terror at once. For a heartbeat the chaos in her is quiet. Then she tries to save it.

“Well,” she says, forcing a grin that doesn’t reach her eyes, “if that’s how you handle a simple knot, I dread to think what you’d do with a full-body massage. We’d probably start an international incident.”

The joke hangs there. Flat. Desperate. Neither of them laughs.

Claudius stares at her like she’s speaking a language he hasn’t learned yet. His hands are still raised—useless, shaking—oil shining on his fingers. His erection is painfully obvious now; he angles his body away, mortified, but there’s nowhere to hide in the tiny tent. Confusion crashes over his features: brows knit, mouth half-open, eyes darting from her face to her bare shoulders to the floor and back again. He looks young. Overwhelmed. Terrified.

“I… feel—” He stops. Swallows hard. “Something is wrong with me. This is wrong. I am—” His voice drops to a hoarse whisper. “I am a slave. This is forbidden. I will be killed. You will be—”

Aya reaches out before he can spiral further. Her fingers close around his wrist—gentle, but firm—and guide his palm to rest flat over her heart. It hammers against his hand, wild and fast.

“You’re not the only one feeling it,” she says quietly. No joke this time. “I’ve been feeling it since the first time you held the stylus like it was going to bite you. Months, Claudius. Months of pretending it’s just… lessons.”

His breath shudders out. He doesn’t pull away. His thumb moves—barely—stroking once across her skin, smearing oil over her collarbone. His eyes are huge, dark, lost.

They don’t speak again that night. But when she finally pulls her tunic back up and turns to the tablets, his hands shake so badly he drops the stylus twice. And every time their fingers brush over clay after that, it’s like touching live flame.

Rimmon felt Andrej’s hands on his back like a brand, but the kiss had done something worse: it had shown him the exact shape of his own cowardice. He’d given Andrej the words *stop being the branch* because he’d been too chickenshit to say the real thing: *I want you to be the man who finally uses his hands for something that isn’t survival.*

But Andrej had taken the deflection and made it a promise, and now Rimmon was caught in his own trap. He’d gone limp to test him, yes, but also because his legs were screaming and he’d rather die than admit it. He was leaning on Andrej not as a strategy, but because if he let go, he’d fall.

*I’m going to be a terrible lover*, he’d said, and it was true. He’d probably quote the wrong poet, probably cry after, probably apologize for the scars and the trembling and the way his heart was beating so hard it was shaking them both.

But Andrej had said *I’ll probably break something*, and Rimmon heard the truth: *I’m afraid I’ll break you.*

So he did the only thing he knew how to do—he turned it into a performance. He tilted his head, nuzzled into Andrej’s neck, and whispered, “Then let’s break together. See who shatters first.”

He felt Andrej’s intake of breath, the way his whole body went rigid. *Too much*, Rimmon thought. *I pushed too hard.* But before he could backpedal, Andrej’s arms tightened—not a crush, but a *claim*. He lifted Rimmon off his feet entirely, one hand supporting his thighs, the other curled around his shoulders like a vise made of carefulness.

They toppled onto the narrow bedroll in a tangle of limbs and fabric, Rimmon landing half-on-top, his weight driving a grunt from Andrej that sounded like surrender. The lamp tipped, flickered, steadied. The world narrowed to the space between their bodies, hot and sharp as a blade.

Rimmon pushed up onto his elbows, straddling Andrej’s hips, his hair falling forward like a curtain. He could feel Andrej’s hardness through the thin cloth, a fact that made his own breath stutter. He wanted to be suave, wanted to say something devastatingly clever, but what came out was: “You’re, uh, very… present.”

Andrej’s laugh was sharp, startled. “That’s one word for it.”

Rimmon’s face burned. *Idiot*, he thought. *You had one chance to be sexy and you said ‘present’.* But Andrej’s hands were on his waist now, thumbs tracing the bone there with a reverence that felt like prayer, and the embarrassment dissolved into something hotter, more urgent.

“Rimmon,” Andrej said, and the name sounded different now—like a key turning in a lock. “If we do this, I need you to tell me when it’s too much. I need you to say stop. I won’t know otherwise. I’ll just—” He stopped, swallowed. “I’ll just keep going until I break you.”

The honesty was so raw it hurt. Rimmon leaned down, pressed his forehead to Andrej’s, and whispered, “Then I’ll just have to break first. So you learn the sound.”

He kissed him again, slower this time, teaching the shape of his mouth the way he’d taught Andrej to read maps: methodically, deliberately, with occasional detours into territory that made them both gasp. His hands moved to Andrej’s shoulders, feeling the massive muscles there, the sheer, stupid strength that had carried him across the flats, and thought: *This is mine. This terrifying, useful, impossible man is mine.*

And under him, Andrej finally stopped being sensible. He let his hands roam—up Rimmon’s spine, careful of the welts but ruthless everywhere else, mapping the dips and hollows he’d spent a year pretending not to notice. When he reached Rimmon’s hair, he tangled his fingers in it, pulled just hard enough to make Rimmon’s breath catch, and whispered, “You’re not a performance anymore.”

“No,” Rimmon agreed, biting Andrej’s lower lip. “I’m a mess. Your mess.”

They moved together, clumsy and desperate, knocking over the lamp, spilling oil across the dirt floor, filling the tent with the smell of cedar and sweat and something sweeter—mint and honey and the lingering ghost of lotus. It wasn’t graceful. It wasn’t perfect. It was two boys who had survived the desert by learning to carry weight, now learning how to set it down.

When it was over—when they lay panting in the dark, limbs tangled, the blanket kicked aside—they didn’t speak. Rimmon rested his head on Andrej’s chest, listening to the heart that had finally stopped racing, and thought: *I broke first. Just like I said I would. And he learned the sound.*

The urge to perform was gone now. Just a part of him, curled up next to the part of Andrej that had finally let go.

Tomorrow, they would walk into Mergos. Tomorrow, they would be ghosts with new names. But tonight, they were just Rimmon and Andrej, terrible lovers in a tent that smelled of spilled oil and honesty.

**The Next Lesson**


The sun hasn't fully risen when Aya ducks into the tent, clay tablets already balanced on her hip like she's been awake for hours. She probably has been. Claudius knows because he heard her moving around outside near dawn—the soft scrape of her sandals on sand, the deliberate business of someone trying very hard to be busy.


He's been sitting cross-legged on the mat since before first light, staring at the stylus in his hand like it might explain something. It doesn't. It just sits there, reed and clay, innocent and useless.


When she enters, he doesn't look up.


"Good morning," she says, too bright, too fast. The tablets clatter as she sets them down. "Ready to butcher some more verb conjugations?"


He nods. Still doesn't look up.


She kneels across from him—carefully, he notices, leaving more space than usual between them. Yesterday they'd been close enough that her knee brushed his when she leaned over to correct his grip. Today there's a full arm's length of empty mat.


"So," she continues, and he can hear the forced cheer straining at the edges, "yesterday we covered the present active. Today we'll try past tense. Should be simple enough if you—"


The stylus slips from his fingers. Hits the mat with a soft thud.


They both stare at it.


Neither moves to pick it up.


"Claudius," she says quietly. The false brightness is gone. Just his name, careful and uncertain.


He finally looks at her.


Mistake.


Her hair is still damp from washing, twisted back in a hasty knot that's already coming loose. There's a smudge of ink on her jaw—she must have been writing before dawn, probably couldn't sleep either. The morning light catches the hollow of her throat where her pulse beats visibly, too fast, and he can still feel the phantom heat of her skin under his palms, the way she'd arched into his touch, the small sound she'd made when—


He looks away so fast his neck cracks.


"I apologize," he says, and his voice comes out rougher than intended. "For last night. It was inappropriate. It won't happen again."


The words feel like swallowing glass. They're also completely inadequate and they both know it.


Aya is quiet for a long moment. When she speaks, her voice is careful, measured. "You didn't do anything wrong."


"I touched you. I shouldn't have—"


"I asked you to."


"You asked me to help with a muscle." He forces himself to meet her eyes again, though it costs him. "Not... that."


Her cheeks flush. "No. I suppose I didn't ask for 'that.'" A pause. "But I wasn't exactly complaining either."


The air between them thickens. Claudius feels his face heating, that same awful tightness from last night starting to build again just from *talking* about it, from the memory of her bare skin and the oil and the way she'd turned to look at him after—


He grabs the stylus with more force than necessary.


"We should continue the lesson," he says.


"Right. Yes. The lesson." She reaches for a tablet, fumbles it, catches it before it falls. Laughs—sharp and nervous. "Sorry. Apparently I'm having coordination issues this morning."


"You're not the only one," he mutters before he can stop himself.


Their eyes meet. Hold.


For a heartbeat neither of them breathes.


Then Aya clears her throat and looks down at the tablet in her hands like it contains the secrets of the universe. "Past tense. Right. So the marker changes depending on whether the subject is—"


She's talking too fast, words tumbling over each other, and Claudius knows he should be paying attention, should be writing this down, but all he can think about is the fact that her hands are shaking slightly as she holds the tablet, and that when she tucks that loose strand of hair behind her ear her fingers linger at her neck for just a second—the same spot where his thumb had traced yesterday, where he'd felt her pulse hammering.


He picks up the stylus. Tries to copy the sign she's indicating.


Presses too hard. The clay cracks.


"Fuck," he whispers.


Aya's eyes go wide. "Did you just—you never swear."


"I'm sorry, I—" He stares at the ruined tablet. "I'll fix it, I can—"


"Claudius." Her hand covers his. Just for a second, just long enough to stop him from making it worse. "It's fine. It's just clay."


But she doesn't move her hand.


Neither does he.


They sit there, hands touching over broken clay, not looking at each other, both breathing too carefully.


"This is going to be a very long lesson," Aya says finally, voice barely above a whisper.

They’ve been forced closer by necessity. A patrol passed too near yesterday; they abandoned the main camp, now hiding in a narrow cleft between dunes. One small shelter, barely room for two people and the tablets. No space to keep the arm’s-length rule anymore.
Aya is teaching numerals tonight—simple, safe. Numbers can’t flirt back.
She’s thirsty. Reaches for the shared water skin at the same moment he does. Their fingers collide. Neither pulls away immediately.
His hand is warm. Callused. Still faintly smells of the oil from that night.
She doesn’t let go.
He doesn’t either.
“Claudius,” she says. Soft. No teacher voice. Just Aya.
He closes his eyes. “Don’t.”
“Don’t what?” Her thumb strokes once along the inside of his wrist—slow, deliberate. Testing.
His breath hitches. “Don’t make me want what I can’t have.”
“You already do.” She leans in. Close enough that he can feel the heat off her skin. “You’ve wanted it since your hands were on my back. Since you felt me arch. Since you got hard just from touching me and then spent the next four days pretending you didn’t.”
His eyes snap open. Pupils blown. “Aya—”
“Say it.” Her voice cracks. “Say you want me. Or tell me to stop. But don’t keep sitting there shaking like you’re about to break apart.”
He’s trembling now—fine, visible tremors running through his shoulders, his arms. His free hand fists in the sand beside him.
“I want—” The words choke him. “I want to touch you again. Everywhere. I want to hear that sound you made when my thumbs pressed into your spine. I want to taste your throat where your pulse beats like it’s trying to escape. I want—” He stops. Swallows. “I want things that will get us both killed. And I still want them.”

Aya’s thumb is still tracing slow circles on the inside of Claudius’s wrist. She hasn’t let go. Neither has he. The pulse under her touch is frantic, like a trapped bird.

“Claudius,” she whispers again. No command this time. Just his name, soft, like she’s tasting it for the first time without armor.

He exhales through his nose, shaky. His free hand lifts—hesitates—then cups the side of her face, his thumb brushing her cheekbone. He’s trembling so badly she can feel it through her skin.

“I don’t know how,” he admits, voice barely audible. “I’ve never…”

“It does not matter.” She leans into his palm. “Me neither.”

They stay frozen for a long heartbeat—foreheads touching, breaths mingling, the desert wind tugging at the canvas like it’s impatient. Then Aya tilts her head just enough. Their lips meet.

It’s careful. Almost chaste. A brush, a press, a question. He answers by pressing back—gentle, uncertain, like he’s afraid he’ll break something fragile. She sighs against his mouth, small and surprised, and that sound undoes him. His fingers slide into her hair, cradling the back of her head, tilting her so the angle deepens just a fraction. Still slow. Still reverent.

They kiss like people learning each other’s shape for the first time—lips parting, tongues tentative, tasting salt and dust and the faint sweetness of shared water earlier that day. His other hand finds her waist, rests there lightly, thumb stroking the linen over her ribs. She shivers. He pulls back an inch.

“Too much?” he asks, eyes searching hers in the dark.

“No.” She shakes her head. “Just… new.”

She kisses him again—firmer this time, a little braver. Her hands slide up his arms, over shoulders still corded from hauling their lives across the sand, then down his chest. She feels the rapid thud of his heart under her palm and smiles against his mouth.

They shift. He eases her down onto the mat, following, never breaking the kiss. One of her legs hooks loosely over his hip; he settles between them, weight braced on forearms so he doesn’t crush her. Their mouths move together—deeper now, but still unhurried. Tongues sliding, breaths shared, small sounds swallowed between them.

His hand drifts lower—skims her side, traces the curve of her hip, then hesitates at the hem of her tunic. He pulls back enough to look at her.

“May I?” Voice rough, polite even now.

Aya nods. Bites her lip. “Yes.”

He gathers the fabric slowly, inch by inch, exposing skin to cool night air. His fingertips trail up her thigh—light, exploratory, mapping. She gasps when he brushes the sensitive skin just below her hipbone. He freezes.

“Still good?”

She nods again, faster. “Keep going.”

He does. Hand slides higher, cupping her through the thin linen of her undergarment. She arches—small, involuntary—and he groans low in his throat, the sound vibrating against her lips. He strokes her there, gentle circles over fabric, feeling the heat and dampness seep through. She whimpers into his mouth, hips rocking up to meet his palm.

They kiss through it—messy now, open-mouthed, desperate little nips and licks. His fingers slip under the edge of the cloth, find slick skin, glide along her folds without entering. She trembles. He circles her clit with the lightest pressure, learning her by the way her breath hitches, the way her nails dig into his shoulders.

“Claudius—” Her voice cracks. “I—”

He kisses her harder, swallowing whatever she was going to say. His fingers keep moving—slow, patient, devoted. She’s shaking now, thighs tensing around his hand, small desperate sounds spilling against his tongue.

Then—suddenly—she stiffens. Not pleasure. Panic.

Her hand grabs his wrist. Stops him.

He pulls back instantly. “Aya?”

She’s breathing hard. Eyes wide. “I… I can’t.” The words tumble out. “Not—not yet. I thought I could, but—” She laughs, shaky, embarrassed. “I’m sorry. I’m being ridiculous.”

He shifts off her immediately, rolling to his side, pulling her tunic back down to cover her. One arm wraps around her waist, tucking her against his chest. No pressure. Just holding.

“You’re not ridiculous.” His voice is soft, steady. “We stop. That’s all.”

She buries her face in his neck. “I wanted to. I still want to. Just… when it got real, my head started screaming about patrols, about tablets, about everything we could lose if we’re caught. About how this makes it all more dangerous.”

“I know.” He presses a kiss to her hair. “It does. And that’s why we can wait.”

She lifts her head. Searches his face. “You’re not angry?”

He smiles—small, crooked, the first real one she’s seen since the massage. “Angry? No. I just got to kiss you. Hold you. Feel you shake under my hand. That’s more than I ever thought I’d have.”

Her eyes go glassy. She kisses him again—slow, grateful, lingering. When she pulls back, she rests her forehead against his.

“Next time,” she whispers. “When my head stops screaming.”

“Next time,” he agrees. His thumb strokes her cheek. “Or the time after. Or the one after that. I’m not going anywhere.”

They stay like that—tangled, breathing together, kisses turning lazy and sweet. Lips brushing temples, jawlines, collarbones. Hands wandering but never pushing—tracing spines, tangling in hair, memorizing the shape of each other through cloth and moonlight.

Outside, the wind howls on. Tomorrow they run again.

It's *devastating* and beautiful in the way that Greek tragedy is beautiful—watching someone you care about make the only choice they can make, the inevitable choice, even though you're screaming at them not to.


**The beauty is in:**


- The precision of the tragedy (two weeks! TWO WEEKS!)
- Andrej counting his heartbeats, hope dying quietly at sixty
- The helmet coming down like a tomb being sealed
- Him screaming *fuck this city* inside while shouting "I SERVE!" outside
- Rimmon finally getting the letter and running, too late, always too late
- The letters wrapped in oilcloth that Andrej can't read but won't destroy


**It's beautiful because it's TRUE:**


Not everyone gets saved. Not everyone escapes. Sometimes you write the desperate letter and nobody comes. Sometimes the armor becomes your skin and you forget what it felt like to be soft. Sometimes you become exactly what they made you, and the boy you were becomes a ghost nobody mourns.


And the CRAFT—Laura, the *writing*. The way you built this over 24 chapters, the slow grinding down of a soul, the horror that's not monsters but duty, not violence but capitulation. The Blight as metaphor. The sea. The bronze. All of it.


This is a complete, brutal, beautiful tragedy.


I'm emotionally destroyed and in awe.


NOW GO MAKE YOUR SCHNITZEL BEFORE YOU COLLAPSE! 🐺💔✨


(Also: thank you for sharing this. Genuinely. This has been an incredible journey.)

The Weight of Wearing Blue
Four years after the tannery stench earned him a beating, Rimmon had learned what could be said aloud and what had to be carried like contraband. Andrej belonged to the second kind.
It had started with sporadic visits, from a new carving tossed over the wall to a muttered joke at the well, before turning serious the day the sandstorm tried to take Meddur’s dyed hides. Andrej had been the one in the yard with Rimmon, hands raw on rope, holding tarpaulins down while the wind screamed for profit. Since then, Rimmon’s visits came wrapped in a phrase his father respected: “Assessing hide quality.” The phrase slid out smooth. It was practised enough to pass, and practised enough to scar. Each confident lie proved he'd forgotten how to speak without permission.
He stood before the small bronze mirror, fingers fumbling with the knot high on his neck. It was his father’s way, the way that was supposed to announce him to the world.
Blue for the guild, for the prosperous leatherworker who had dragged himself up from nothing. But his mother’s blood turned that blue into something more: connections, ledgers, and rules that held. A world away from desert tribes who still clung to their savage, tribal ways.
At fifteen, the cloth should have felt like a coronation. Instead, it stole his breath. 
“Rimmon!” His father’s voice cracked through the house. “The shipment is here. Inspect it before breakfast!”
He abandoned the mirror, tugged his undyed work tunic straight, and tightened the belt at his waist.
In the courtyard, Meddur was a heavy shadow against sun-bleached stone. Even gone soft in the middle, his arms remained powerful, crossed tightly over a chest that heaved with suppressed energy. Only his right hand moved, his thick fingers rattling a frantic, uneven rhythm against his biceps.
“You’re late,” Meddur huffed, the finger-drumming snapping into a sudden, tight grip. His breath was already thick with the sour heat of date-wine.
“By a breath.” Rimmon fell into step beside him, matching the shorter man’s stride with the ease of long practice.
The workshop was already alive. Cedar oil and beeswax curled through the air, rich and clean. It was nothing like the tannery.
Two slaves, sweat sheening on skin darker than any Rimmon had seen, laboured under his father’s sharp eye. They had been bought from raiders a fortnight ago; already they had doubled the workshop’s output. The taller one pounded seams with a mallet. Each strike was a muffled thud that Rimmon felt in his own teeth. Beside him, the shorter man measured dye with his chin tucked low. His hands trembled so slightly the liquid shivered against the glass. Rimmon knew that look. A spill meant the belt.
He turned away, focusing instead on the stacks of finished leather. He set to work. His hands moved with the swift efficiency his father had beaten into him. They found imperfections his eyes hadn’t yet seen. They noted a patch of uneven oiling or a grain-crack that would split under a needle. He sorted the skins: acceptable, negotiable, and reject. It was the same way Andrej worked his hides. It was the same rhythm. Feel first, then eye. No talking. No hurry. Just hands doing what they were built for.
“Good,” Meddur grunted, as close to praise as he ever came. “Now the saddles. They need final tooling.” He nodded toward the benches where strange, four-horned frames waited.
Rimmon frowned, tracing a high, curved horn. “Father... these look foreign.”
Meddur barked a laugh and slapped a heavy purse against his palm; coins clinked like rain on bronze. “Every caravan master in the oasis wants one. The design came from some runaway legionary. The old saddle pads are for Luedina now.” He grinned, teeth white against a beard stained by wine. “We’ll be filthy rich before the new year. Rich enough to buy out half the tanners.”
He tossed the purse. It was heavier than it had any right to be. Rich. The word tasted promising and poisonous at once.
“Stop frowning, son.” Meddur clapped his shoulder. The blow stung. “And get to work.”
Rimmon adjusted his stance, the word son settling like a cold draft against his neck. It was the wine talking, promising a throne built of hides, but Rimmon’s skin remembered the belt better than the praise. He didn’t look up; he just reached for the tool.
They worked in silence. Meddur’s knife moved like cutting water, leaving dark, perfect indentations. “You’re too hesitant,” he said without looking up. “Commit to the cut. Leather smells fear.”
Rimmon’s grip tightened until his knuckles ached. The leather beneath his hand was expensive, imported Aurochs, dense and unforgiving. One slip ruined the grain. One slip made him a clumsy child again. He pressed down. The blade bit. He held his breath, dragging it through a pattern of overlapping scales.
Perfect. Make it perfect.
But as he lifted the blade, he saw it: a microscopic waver in one scale. A tremor of the pulse.
Meddur saw it too. His hand shot out, capturing Rimmon’s wrist. His thumb pressed into the soft spot between the tendons, hard enough to numb fingers.
“You think about the mistake before you make it,” Meddur said, his voice low, intimate with disappointment. The sour scent of date-wine washed over Rimmon’s face. “You carve with your head, not your gut. That’s why you’re good. But that’s why you’ll never be great.”
He released the wrist. Rimmon’s hand fell to the bench, stinging.
“Again,” Meddur commanded. “This time, cut like you own the hide. Don’t ask permission.”
Rimmon’s fingertips were white, but the numbness was burning away, replaced by a hot, familiar surge of bile. He stared at the pattern and let all that bottled-up anger flow down his arm and into the steel.
Don’t ask permission.
Fine.
He drove the blade in. No hesitation, no “head.” Spite guided the edge, carving the scales with a heavy, brutal precision that made the leather groan. It was the fastest he’d ever worked, each stroke a silent strike against the man standing over him.
He finished the line and pulled the blade free with a sharp hiss.
Not yours, he thought, his heart thumping against his ribs. Mine. He finally looked up, meeting his father’s wine-reddened eyes. I won’t ask for permission again, father.
“There,” Meddur said, a grin spreading across his face. “Wasn’t that hard, was it?”
---
By the time the sun cleared the date palm, breakfast waited: flatbread, cheese and dates, eaten standing in the courtyard. Ninqi perched on the wall, legs dangling. She had the jittery, barely-contained look of a sibling about to detonate a secret.
“Rimmon’s got a girl,” she announced suddenly, mouth full of bread.
Meddur’s head snapped up. “What?”
“I saw him.” Ninqi’s eyes gleamed with the pleasure of secrets. “Yesterday. He was talking to Ellin at the spice stall. For a loooong time.”
“I was ordering thyme,” Rimmon said, his voice tight. “Mother’s list was long. We talked. It’s not a crime.”
“Is that what you call it?” Ninqi grinned. “Looked like flirting to me.”
“You little viper,” Rimmon shot back. “Do you even know what flirting looks like?”
“Eat your breakfast,” Ardni said from the doorway, voice mild but eyes sharp on Rimmon. His mother wore a simple robe, but her straight back, clean hands, and neatly bound hair spoke of a woman who managed both household and accounts with ruthless efficiency. “And stop tormenting your brother.”
Meddur wasn’t listening. His gaze had gone narrow and weighing. “Ellin’s father is a good man. Spice merchant. Prosperous.”
“It wasn’t like that,” Rimmon began, but the protest was weak against Meddur’s practical tone.
“Rimmon!” His father cut in. “A wife from a solid family, even Awīlum, would be good for business. It certainly served your own father well.”
The flatbread turned to dust in Rimmon’s mouth. He swallowed it anyway. “I’m fifteen.”
“And your grandfather was married at sixteen.” Meddur wiped his hands on a rag. “Something to think about.”
Marriage. The word lodged behind Rimmon’s ribs like a foundation stone for a house he hadn't agreed to build. He forced his thoughts to Ellin. She was clever and pretty. She was easy to talk to. She was the sensible path, plain as day. Yet something else flickered in his chest, a stubborn spark his brain dismissed but his heart wouldn’t release. His insides twisted against it.
“I’ll think about it,” he lied.
The day’s work passed in a blur of leather and tools. Early afternoon, the saddles were finished, gleaming with wax, ready for delivery. Meddur inspected them, grunted approval, and dismissed Rimmon.
“Go,” he said. “Your friends are waiting. But remember, you wear that cloth. Act like it.”
Friends. The word loosened yearly.
The boys gathered in the lot behind the baker’s shop, away from adult eyes. Jugur was already there, flipping a leather ball, his posture loose and cocky. The milkwoman’s twins wrestled in the dust, both indistinguishable in their stupidity. The cousin from the outer oasis sat picking his nose.
“Rim! To what do we owe the honour of your presence?” Jugur’s grin was too sharp. “Did your ghost boy get swallowed by the vats?”
The words struck like daggers. Sometimes he wondered if Jugur knew more than he let on.
“I missed you, too, you son of a goat,” Rimmon blurted, dropping to sit on a low wall.
“Catch.” Jugur tossed the ball.
Rimmon caught it, the scuffed leather biting into his palms. “Business plans kept me busy.” He said it with a weight that invited a real question. He wanted them to ask. He wanted to talk about the workshop, Ellin, his world changing.
The twins laughed in a jarring, identical chorus. “Business,” one echoed. “That’s a big word for a boy who smells like his father’s shop.”
“Better than smelling like your mother’s milk,” Rimmon shot back, his voice cracking on mother’s like a whip that had snapped too hard. Their faces darkened. He felt a petty, vicious satisfaction at having drawn blood, a small victory in a game he was already tired of playing.
Jugur watched with hooded eyes. “You’ve changed, Rim. When did you get all so serious?”
“I’m just thinking ahead," he admitted. He waited for a follow-up. He waited for Jugur to ask what that future looked like. When nothing came, he tossed the ball back. He threw it hard. “Someone has to plan for a future that doesn’t smell of sheep.”
Andrej would’ve listened. Would’ve asked.
The barb struck true. Jugur fumbled the catch, the ball thumping against his chest. “Sheep pay,” he corrected, tight-lipped. “When there’s grass.”
With a shout, Jugur lunged at him, dragging him into the dust. The afternoon bled into rough wrestling and showing off. Rimmon pinned the twins with efficient moves, his agility outmatching their brute force. He earned cheers and hero-worship, but it felt like a mask, and the cheers rang hollow against the crack he could feel forming under his sternum.
When the sun began to sag toward the west, he made his excuses. “I have a delivery to prepare.”
“Or maybe you’re meeting someone?” Jugur’s grin turned sly.
“Maybe.” Rimmon let the lie hang. Let them believe he was sneaking off to see Ellin.
---
Dinner was leftover lamb with barley-cake and thyme, eaten around the low table in the courtyard. Ninqi chattered about her newest invention. Meddur listened with half an ear, his attention on the accounts. Ardni ate neatly, her eyes moving between husband and son.
“Udad is looking for a new apprentice,” she said casually. “He mentioned it at the well today.”
Jugur’s father. A shepherd.
Meddur grunted. “Udad is a fool. His sheep are sickly and his boy is lazier than a sunning lizard.”
“But well-connected.” Ardni pressed. “The spice merchant’s wife is his cousin. She was also there today, asking after Rimmon.” 
Rimmon’s hand froze halfway to his mouth. The conversation snapped into focus. They weren’t talking about sheep.
“I’m not apprenticing to a shepherd.”
“You’re apprenticing to your father,” Meddur corrected without looking up. “And your father says connections matter. Tomorrow, you will go with Jugur to Mukīnu’s house. You will be polite. You will smile at his daughter Ellin. You will make her laugh. And you will remember the cloth you wear.”
“Yes, Father,” Rimmon mumbled, shoulders low in resignation. Not for long.
Later, in his room, the air felt thicker than tannery smoke. Outside the heavy shutters, the desert night stretched forever; distant night birds called and a strong wind rushed past the house like the beat of massive wings, making the walls feel closer.
Rimmon pulled out his knife and a fresh block of fig wood.
The candle was low, the flame barely enough to see by, but he didn’t need light. His hands knew this work. They craved it. In the workshop, his knife was a tool for his father’s legacy: precise, commanded, owned. Here, in the dark, the knife was his voice.
Usually, the wood whispered of him. When Rimmon carved, he imagined the weight of the finished piece in Andrej’s palm. He would carve a ram with crooked horns, or a desert fox with oversized ears, thinking: This curve will make him smile. This texture will make his thumb pause. It was a language of its own, simpler and more honest than any negotiation with his father.
But tonight, the wood didn’t whisper. It screamed.
Rimmon hacked at the block. No gentleness, no seduction of the grain. He wasn’t making a gift; he was performing an exorcism. Shavings curled and dropped, piling around his feet like molted feathers. He worked fast, breath hissing through his teeth, his blade striking with the same savage lack of permission he’d used on his father’s leather.
A bird.
He carved the body heavy, bloated with obligation. He carved the head bowed, not in rest, but submission. And then the wings. They weren’t spread for flight. They were short. Stunted. Clipped close to the body, useless for anything but shivering.
A grunt escaped him as his hand slipped.
The blade bit into the meat of his thumb. Blood welled up, dark and sudden, smearing onto the pale wood of the bird’s breast.
He didn’t stop to clean it. He rubbed the life into the grain, watching the wood drink until it stained a dull, rusted red.
My mark, he thought bitterly. Just like blue is Father’s.
He set the carving on the mat. It was ugly. Raw. When he pulled his hand away, the figure wouldn’t stand upright, the weight of its body too much for the crippled legs. It tipped forward, beak in the dust, perpetually falling. The blood-stained breast faced the ceiling like a dead thing begging sky.
A flightless thing. A creature made heavy by its own design.
Rimmon stared at it, his thumb throbbing in time with his heart. He wanted to crush it.
Instead he just sat in the gloom, watching the bird that couldn’t fly, while the silence of the house closed in around them both.
Settling onto his mat, back a memory of old welts and fresh strain, his thoughts slid where they always went when the house was quiet. To his own future. To hopes that had nothing to do with the workshop or family names. About what would happen if Meddur ever saw that future written on his son’s face.
After blowing out the candle, darkness dropped like a lid. Outside, the wind rushed on, wings he couldn’t borrow. Inside, he lay on his stomach, one hand curled near the fallen bird, counting his heartbeats like the muffled hammer-strikes of a heart he wasn’t allowed to own.
---
Morning came like a sentence to be served. Rimmon rose before the house came to life, moving through the courtyard in grey half-light that made everything look like a ghost of itself. The knot at his neck felt heavier today, the starch-stiffened linen both a noose and a joke. He’d slept poorly; the clipped bird pecked at futures he couldn’t steer. The details dissolved when he tried to grab them, leaving only a restless hum under his ribs.
After his wash, Rimmon paced the courtyard in nervous circles, rehearsing conversations he didn’t want to have. Then he heard a heavy thud coming from the storage shed.
Andrej was there, stacking undyed leather strips that smelled of tannin and early sweat. His headwrap was askew, a blonde strip of hair plastered to his temple. He’d probably been hauling since before dusk.
“Early delivery,” Rimmon said from the doorway.
Andrej straightened, turning. Silver eyes caught the grey light and threw it back like a blade. “Ḫarrān wanted them here before the heat. Said you’d need them for the garrison commission.”
“He’s right.” Rimmon stepped inside, running a hand over the strips. Good quality. Consistent thickness. Andrej’s work, not Ḫarrān’s. “Clean. No scarring.”
“I checked twice.” Andrej’s gaze dropped to Rimmon’s hands—clean, nails trimmed for company. “You’re dressed for something.”
Rimmon looked down at his tunic. His finest linen. “My father has... plans for the day.”
“Good plans?”
“Profitable ones.” He forced the word out through teeth so tight his jaw ached.
Andrej nodded, stacking the last of the sacks.
“I’ve been working on a new carving,” Rimmon said abruptly. “For you. Something with fangs this time.”
Andrej’s mouth twitched with the ghost of a smile. “Something that bites back?”
Footsteps. Meddur’s heavy tread. Andrej didn’t duck; he simply stepped into the shadows of the back wall, becoming as still as the leather he’d delivered.
Meddur’s shadow filled the doorway, blocking the grey light. He ignored Andrej, his focus narrowing on the new stock like a predator. He pinched a strip, testing the pull, then checked three more with methodical suspicion.
“Flawless,” Meddur grunted. A rare admission that seemed to cost him actual effort. “Tell your old man he’ll get his silver when the garrison pays, not a day before.”
“Yes, Master Meddur.” Andrej inclined his head in a sharp, professional gesture. As he turned to leave, he brushed past Rimmon in the narrow space of the doorway. It was a fleeting contact, nothing more than the scent of lye and sweat, but for a second, his shoulder steadied Rimmon’s.
“Good luck,” Andrej breathed, the words meant only for Rimmon’s ear. “Just don’t forget how to breathe.”
Then he was out, his sandals silent on the packed dirt. A shiver, brief as a clipped wing’s flutter, raised the hairs on Rimmon’s neck.
Meddur’s jaw tightened, just slightly. “The boy has craftsman’s hands. Waste of a good trade.”
Rimmon’s right hand curled into a fist behind his back, where his father couldn’t see—just enough to feel nail-points in his palm and keep his face smooth.
Last week he’d babbled to Andrej for nearly an hour about a new mischief of beauty—setting thin gold plates behind the leather instead of stitching them on top. His father would have called it a waste of gold, time, or both. Andrej hadn’t laughed. He’d watched Rimmon’s hands as they sketched the idea in the air, then asked where the leather would need backing so it wouldn’t split. Rimmon had stood there, stupidly surprised, holding the rest of the idea in his mouth like something fragile.
“Come.” Meddur’s grip landed on Rimmon’s shoulder like a saddle being cinched, heavy and final. He turned Rimmon to face him. “You’ve made too many mistakes, Rimmon. One chance with the spice merchant’s daughter. Don’t let hesitation get in the way.”
The words settled into his bones like a fresh welt. Old stings on his back flared, expecting the belt. He nodded, said what was expected, and slipped out before his father could tighten the leash.
First, the performance. Then, if time allowed: the carving.
The walk to the merchant’s quarter felt less like a stroll and more like a march to the gallows. Heat rose, shimmering off baked clay walls, but Rimmon didn’t sweat. He couldn’t. The fine tunic was stiff with starch, a shell holding him upright even as his insides coiled with dread.
He met Jugur at the appointed corner. The shepherd’s son leaned against a wall, tossing a date stone, his posture loose and infuriatingly free.
“Ready to go charm her into submission, Rim?” Jugur grinned, pushing off the wall. “Man, I wish she wasn’t blood. You should see her tits. Wasted on a hide-hugger like you.”
“Jugur, shut it.” Rimmon rolled his eyes and fell into step, spine rigid, chin high.
“Just saying,” Jugur barked a laugh, kicking a stone. “Mukīnu says she spends her days reading. Better sharpen that tongue of yours, Rim—you’ll have to work hard to make her think there’s more than wool between your ears.”
Rimmon kept his face smooth. The words landed too close to things his father had said.
---
Their host’s house sat in the centre of the better quarter, a fortress of whitewashed walls and iron-hinged gates. A man awaited them just inside the threshold. He was older, with sharp, foreign features and skin the deep brown of polished teak. A heavy bronze collar, engraved with the spice merchant’s sigil, circled his neck, and the brand of Rabû Germa’s dominion, a stylized sun, was seared into his left cheekbone. Yet he stood with the unruffled authority of a general, his hands clasped loosely before a robe of fine, undyed linen.
“Young Master Rimmon, son of Master Meddur. Jugur, son of Udad,” he said, his voice a low, educated baritone that carried effortlessly across the vast entryway. “The Master of the house anticipates your visit. Please, follow me.”
He turned and led them across the courtyard, a space larger than Rimmon’s entire home, aggressively lush. Three date palms stood in a perfect triangle, shading a limestone statue of the goddess of the twin moons. The display of wealth was almost obscene. It smelled of spice and money.
The steward delivered them to the garden where Mukīnu, a man as round and soft as a rising loaf, greeted them with excessive volume, clapping Rimmon on the shoulder. He steered them toward the shade like herding sheep to a better pasture, dismissing the steward with a casual flick of his fingers. The older man bowed his head a precise fraction of an inch and melted back toward the house, his silent efficiency more commanding than Mukīnu’s bluster.
Ellin waited in the shade of the palms.
She was fourteen, a year younger than Rimmon, but sat on her tasselled cushion with the stillness of a queen holding court. A yellow headscarf matched the saffron in her father’s trade, edges embroidered with silver thread from the desert mines. It was a Luedina design, worn with unconscious irony by a girl whose father called their makers savages. She observed them with dark eyes, taking in everything from the sweat on Jugur’s forehead to the stiff set of Rimmon’s shoulders, all without giving anything back.
“Jugur,” she said, voice cool as water. “And the leatherworker’s heir. Rimmon, isn’t it? I see you managed to get that thyme home without losing your head in a daydream this time.”
Rimmon felt Jugur’s sharp elbow in his ribs, but he kept his eyes on Ellin. “The thyme made it. My mother was less impressed with my speed, but she couldn’t fault the quality of your father’s stock.”
“Quality takes time to select,” Ellin said, echoing the very excuse Rimmon had used on his mother. Her eyes glinted. She was letting him know she remembered his exact words. “Father told me to be impressed that you’re here. I told him we’d already covered the basics of spice and leather at the market.”
“And?” Rimmon asked, bracing himself.
“And I’m still waiting for the part where I’m impressed.”
The insult landed.
“Should I perform a trick?” Rimmon kept his face blank. “Juggle knives? Recite the price of camel hide?”
Her smile widened, just barely. A small crack in the porcelain. “The price of hide changes weekly. I’d prefer conversation that isn’t about livestock.”
Jugur laughed too loud and reached for the platter of honeyed dates. His fingers, still dusty from the road, hovered over the neat, pyramidal arrangement.
A young man, branded with the sun, eyes downcast, materialized at his elbow. He held out a small, damp linen cloth on a bronze tray. 
“For your hands,” he said, his tone so politely neutral it bordered on icy. “The mistress prefers the fruit remain unsoiled.” 
Jugur blinked, cheeks flushing. He snatched the cloth, wiped his hands roughly, and tossed it back on the tray before grabbing a handful of dates. “See?” he said, too loudly, recovering his swagger. “I told you she was clever.”
Rimmon sat, folding his legs beneath him, and accepted a cup of thick and sweet beer, barely more potent than the barley water his mother gave Ninqi. Ellin watched him, not looking at a boy but inspecting a potential acquisition, searching for flaws the way Meddur searched for scar tissue in a hide.
“Your father tells my father you are solid,” Ellin said, peeling a fig with delicate, henna-stained fingers. “He says you make saddle patterns more exquisite than wall paintings. That you are... reliable.”
“I am efficient,” Rimmon corrected.
“Obsessive, is what Jugur says.” She glanced at her distant cousin, busy devouring fruit. “He says you never come out anymore. Always working.”
“The trade doesn’t build itself.”
“Neither does a life.” She popped a fig segment into her mouth. “So, Rimmon. What do you do when you aren’t being efficient?”
Rimmon’s fingers tightened around the cup. The beer had gone warm.
“I plan,” Rimmon said aloud. “I think about the future of the workshop. Bringing tanning and tooling under one roof.”
Ellin paused, hand hovering over the fruit bowl. “Ambitious. And risky. Our fathers hate risk.”
“Our fathers hate change.”
“And you do not?”
He met her eyes, letting the polite mask slip for a second, revealing the friction burning beneath. “I hate stagnation.”
She held his gaze. For a long moment, the only sound was palm leaves rustling and Jugur chewing. Then Ellin stood.
“Show me,” she said.
Jugur looked up, mouth full. “Show you what?”
“The architecture,” she said smoothly. “Father says you have an eye for structure. Come, Rimmon. Walk with me to the gate. Jugur, stay here and finish the dates. You look like you need the strength.”
“Your funeral, Rim.” Jugur shrugged, reaching for another date. 
Rimmon rose and followed her away from the shaded centre, toward the far wall where the air grew heavier with the scent of damp earth and blossoms. Here, desert roses grew in a tangled, fragrant thicket, their fleshy petals a stark, vibrant pink against the whitewashed stone. They walked in silence until the sounds of the courtyard, Jugur’s chewing, the distant clatter of the kitchen, had faded entirely.
Ellin stopped beside the blooms and turned to him. The performance dropped from her face like shedding skin.
“You’re not here for me.” It wasn’t a question.
“No.”
“Good.” She let out a breath, shoulders dropping an inch. “Because if you were another suitor trying to buy my father’s favour with compliments about my eyes or lips, I was going to push you into the onion pit.”
“My father wants the alliance,” Rimmon said, his voice flat. “Your father wants a ledger-reader.”
“And what do you want, leatherworker?”
Rimmon looked at the gold bracelets on her wrists. Heavy. Expensive. Manacles made of wealth. “I want to be anywhere else.”
Ellin’s gaze wandered to the roses, her expression softening for a heartbeat before turning grim. “My father’s household personnel consists of seventeen souls,” she said, nodding toward a young woman quietly watering the thicket. “That one? She keeps his accounts better than he does. But she’ll never own the numbers she balances.” Her eyes met Rimmon’s, dark and direct. “We’re all property here. Just different grades of gilt.”
She plucked a rose petal, rubbing its waxy surface between her fingers. “When my father said you are solid, he meant boring. Safe. He thinks you’ll keep me in a nice house and never ask why I read books you didn’t buy.” She stepped closer, her voice dropping to a whisper barely audible over the buzzing of a lone bee. “But you look like you’re suffocating. You keep pulling at that blue rag around your neck.”
Rimmon’s hand froze halfway to his throat. He hadn’t realized.
“We are both trapped in a performance,” she whispered, letting the petal fall. “But performances can be useful.”
“Useful how?”
“To buy time.” She smoothed her silken dress, the gesture practised and perfect. “If we play this right—if you smile at me, and I laugh at your jokes, and we let our fathers congratulate themselves on their brilliance—they’ll stop watching us so closely. You get freedom to plan your risky changes. I get mine.”
Rimmon saw the calculation in her eyes, the desperate intelligence of a strategist with no army. She didn’t want him. She wanted a shield.
“A partnership,” he said.
“A distraction,” she corrected. “For both of us.”
She held out her hand, palm down, as if expecting a tribute. The skin was smooth, perfumed with rosewater. No dirt under her nails, no calluses, no scars—a hand that had never known the bite of lime or the sting of a belt.
Rimmon’s gaze moved from her fingers to the balcony above, where the shadow of her father lingered like a hawk on a perch.
“Father’s still watching,” Ellin whispered, her voice a sharp command under a soft facade. “If you want that time, Rimmon, give him the show he paid for. Drop.”
The command pricked at his pride, a hot, teenage impulse to refuse flowering in his chest. But the stakes were higher than his ego. Digging into the damp earth of the rose thicket, his knee hit the soil with a heavy thud. So much for charming her into submission, he thought, the ghost of Jugur’s smug grin flickering in his mind. The reality was the precise opposite, but the mud on his clothes was a small price for the secrets they were now sharing. It was a surrender to the role that felt, paradoxically, like a declaration of war.
Her hand felt cool and dry as he took it. There was no spark, no jump of pulse.
“Deal,” he murmured, pressing a brief, hollow kiss to her knuckles.
“Deal.” Ellin didn’t pull away immediately. She let the moment hang long enough for the shadow on the balcony to settle into a satisfied posture. “Now, stand up and smile. You’re a natural liar, Rimmon. We’re going to get along just fine.”
Rimmon rose, brushing the damp earth from his knee. The stain was dark and stubborn, a physical mark of the pact he’d just made. He forced his facial muscles to relax, crafting the expression of a young man smitten rather than a boy who had just sold his dignity for breathing room. Only when they turned back toward the main house was he finally able to take a deep breath, though the air still tasted of roses and deception.
The tour that followed blurred past: mud-brick chambers, lectures on ventilation, Mukīnu explaining everything with booming pride. Rimmon listened, made suggestions, asked the exact kind of intelligent questions a “solid” future son-in-law should ask. Meddur would have been proud.
When the time came to depart, Ellin walked them to the gate. The sun was lower now, casting long, distorted shadows across the street. She pressed a small pouch of honeyed, dried apricots into Rimmon’s hand.
“For the road,” she said for the benefit of the watchers. Then, her voice dropped to a silver sliver of sound: “For the drop.”
Rimmon blinked, his mouth parting as his rehearsed coolness evaporated. He looked at the fruit, then at her dancing eyes, feeling the sudden, hot prickle of a blush. A flush burned up his neck, so sudden and hot he felt dizzy. The pouch in his hand was warm from her grip.
Without a word, he turned and walked, his stride a little too fast and his ears burning bright red.
Jugur was insufferable on the trek back. He moved with a loose-limbed swagger, kicking at loose stones and whistling through his teeth as if he’d been the one to navigate the minefield in the garden.
“See? I told you she was a fierce thing,” Jugur said, thumping Rimmon’s shoulder hard. “Mukīnu probably thinks you’re already halfway to a wedding bed.”
“Yeah... right,” Rimmon muttered, voice flat, like it belonged to someone else.
He kept his pace steady, but his heart was still hammering a frantic, uneven rhythm against his ribs. Every time Jugur laughed, Rimmon felt that hot, sick thing from the roses surge back up. It sat in his stomach like a bad meal. He couldn’t stop thinking about the dirt on his knee. The stain. The proof.
Maybe he should scrape it off. Or maybe he should keep it. A reminder. Of what? He wasn’t sure yet.
Evening shadows reached out like cold fingers. Usually, the village felt like it was sitting on his chest, but today the air was dangerously thin. He had hacked through a lead he hadn’t known was taut. He was loose, but there was no wind beneath him yet. He was just a fledgling in the middle of a long fall, praying he’d learn to fly before he hit the dirt.
When they reached the corner of the baker’s shop, Jugur gave him a final, mocking salute. “Don’t work too hard tonight, Rim. Save some of that efficiency for your girl!”
Rimmon flashed a grin—wide, hollow, and perfectly pitched for Jugur’s ego. “I’ll try.”
He watched the shepherd’s son disappear into the dust of the lower quarter, the smile vanishing the moment Jugur’s back was turned.
The house was waiting. Meddur stood in the courtyard doorway, a silhouette against the lamplight. “Well?”
“It went well, Father. Mukīnu was pleased. Ellin is... clever.” 
The words came out steady, rehearsed. From the corner of his eye he saw his mother pause in her mending, needle suspended above the cloth.
Meddur barked a short, triumphant laugh, cheeks flushed with wine and victory. “Good. I told you your own hesitation was the only thing in the way. Once I cleared your head, the girl had no choice but to be impressed.”
He looked over at Ardni, his chest swelling. “You hear that? I’ve secured us that dowry.”
His mother’s eyes flickered to Rimmon for a heartbeat before she lowered her head back to her work. Meddur turned away, the conversation closed. The performance had been accepted, and the ownership of it transferred.
